The qualified data-valid predicate now feeds all four consumers consistently,
rejecting cancelled and exception responses. Raw ROB completion/exception reporting
is retained: an exception must still reach precise retirement. No state, queue,
stage, clock/reset, memory macro, ISA/DTS field or default is added. Existing OoOEn
and hart/FP legality restrictions remain the integration boundary.

`ooo-qualification-owner-20260919` checks retained waiters, newly dispatched waiters,
operand bypass after genuine writeback, and exception response availability. Four
positives/four negatives pass; four private restored-defect controls fail. The
fixture had a Verilator5.008 packed-part-update propagation problem, exposed by a
zero PRF value. Whole-vector input drives preserve the intended stimulus and fix
the fixture; PRF RTL is unchanged. Retain that tool limitation, not a PRF repair claim.
Dispatch regression16, rename22 and LSQ24 records pass, and both illegal SMT/FP
elaboration controls still refuse their configurations.

Live-port synthesis exposed an LSQ lane-mask loop with a runtime-dependent bound.
Its equivalent rewrite iterates the fixed XLEN byte lanes and predicates each bit
by access size. With that same rewrite in both synthesis arms, generic cells change
91,242 ->88,501 (-3.00%) for the writeback qualification change; state remains6,250
bits and both arms have zero latches/SCCs. Artifact: ooo-wb-area-v2-{before,after}-20260919.
This is one two-port integer fixture, not mapped silicon area, power or timing. The
qualified-valid gate adds cancellation/exception fanout to wakeup/bypass; no Fmax
or IPC improvement is claimed. The inferred router1.25GHz target still needs STA.

The rename formal task now references checkpoint count/head rather than the removed
pointer: its original12-step BMC passes. A private false-x0 checker yields a concrete
four-step SAT counterexample. These are bounded invariants, not checkpoint lifetime
or full architecture proofs. Fetch-IQ's assertion/witness problem remains separate.

#### CSR and LR results never reach the PRF under OoOEn=1 — CONFIRMED DEFECT (2026-09-19)

This is the gate "actual CSR/AMO late results must reach PRF/wakeup, not early
commit-ready placeholders", now traced to a precise mechanism. Unlike the TID-reuse
item below, no masking invariant was found — and the absence was checked, not assumed.

The chain:

1. `g6lc_ooo_dispatch` writes the PRF from the execute writeback bus only —
   `prf_wdata[w] = wb_data_i[w]`, `prf_we[w] = wb_value_valid[w]`. There is **no
   commit-time PRF write path**.
2. The architectural results of CSR and AMO are substituted at COMMIT, into the
   architectural register file: `commit_stage` sets `wdata_o[0] = csr_rdata_i` for CSR
   and `wdata_o[0] = amo_resp_i.result` for AMO, both overriding
   `commit_instr_i[0].result`. Neither reaches `wb_data_i`.
3. Every dispatched instruction is renamed — `tagged_sbe[p].ooo_renamed = can_go &&
   dispatch_valid_i[p]`, with no exclusion for CSR or AMO — and a renamed consumer
   takes its operands from the PRF (`prf_raddr[...] = ooo_renamed ? p_rs1 : '0`).
4. No commit flush covers the gap for the two worst cases. `commit_stage` asserts
   `flush_commit_o` for SC and RMW but **explicitly not for LR**
   (`if (!ariane_pkg::is_amo_lr(...)) flush_commit_o = amo_resp_i.ack;`), and does not
   assert it for CSR at all.

So under `OoOEn=1` a consumer renamed against a **CSR read** or an **LR** destination
reads the PRF and receives the execute-stage placeholder rather than the architectural
value, with no flush to squash it. This is the same defect class as the in-order LR
bug already repaired (LR's early writeback 0 reaching a dependent branch), which the
in-order fix does not cover for the OoO path.

5. The consumer really does take the PRF value: `issue_read_operands` selects
   `operand_a_regfile[i] = (OoOEn && ooo_op_a_valid_i[i] && ooo_renamed) ? ooo_op_a_i[i]
   : rdata[...]`, so the PRF operand **overrides** the architectural regfile read.

**CONFIRMED in simulation** (`ooo-lateresult-confirmed-20260919`, matched=true against
expected error `DISPATCH_LATERESULT`). `tb_g6lc_review_dispatch` scenario 19
(`REVIEW_RTL_LATERESULT=1`) dispatches a CSR producer, delivers only the placeholder
`0x0BAD` on the execute writeback, commits the architectural `0x1234` as commit_stage
does, then dispatches a consumer renamed against that destination:

**the consumer read 0x0BAD while the architectural value was 0x1234.**

Unlike the TID-reuse item below, this one is real on all three counts: the mechanism is
confirmed in a fixture, the reachability chain is verified end to end in source, and
the candidate masking invariant was searched for and found explicitly absent for
exactly CSR and LR.

**REPAIRED (2026-09-19).** `g6lc_ooo_dispatch` now mirrors the architectural commit
write into the PRF: `issue_stage` feeds it the same `we_gpr_i`/`wdata_i` the
architectural register file receives, and the mirror is keyed by `commit_prd[c]` — the
committing instruction's OWN physical register — rather than by a trans_id that could
already belong to someone else. That ordering matters: keying on a trans_id would
reintroduce exactly the aliasing the TID-reuse invariant below prevents. `g6lc_prf`
lets the highest write port win a same-address clash, so the mirror is appended after
the execute writebacks and architectural truth overrides a coincident placeholder.

Mirroring every committed write, rather than decoding CSR/AMO specifically, keeps the
contract future-proof: whatever the architectural register file receives, the PRF
receives. No exclusivity proof was available for reusing an execute writeback port at
commit, so a port was added — and its cost was measured rather than assumed.

Port count is the cost driver, so the mirror is **one** port, not one per commit port.
Only commit port 0 can carry a value produced at commit (`wdata_o[0]` from
`csr_rdata_i` / `amo_resp_i.result`); port 1 writes `commit_instr[1].result`, which the
execute writeback already delivered. The single exception is the AMOCAS.Q dual write,
so a second mirror port appears only when `RVZacas` is configured (it is 0 here).

| Fixture variant | Generic cells | Latches | SCCs |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Previously recorded baseline | 86,824 | 0 | 0 |
| One mirror port (shipped) | **94,505** | 0 | 0 |
| One per commit port | 97,439 | 0 | 0 |

The 1-vs-2 port figures are a paired measurement taken here; the baseline is quoted
from an earlier run of the same fixture, so treat the absolute delta as indicative and
the 2,934-cell saving from dropping the second port as the directly measured result.
State stays at 6,250 bits in all three. This is a generic-cell fixture, not mapped PPA.

Verification: scenario 19 now passes; `REVIEW_RTL_LATERESULT_FAULT=1` restores the
missing mirror and it fails again, so the scenario is a genuine discriminator.
Scenarios 11-14, 15-17 and the default dispatch set with their negative controls all
still pass, and the TID-reuse probe is unchanged. The `g6lc64_smt2` model rebuilds
**byte-identical** (`ba1f8f6a722829aa…`), confirming the `OoOEn=0` path is untouched,
so the dual-hart boot gate remains bound without re-running it.

#### Historical late-writeback assessment (2026-09-19; superseded)

**The reachability conclusion below is superseded by the pre-grant cancellation
counterexample at the top of this file.** The response-buffer survey omitted the
bypass queue before cache grant. Keep the original probe and observations; do not
reuse this historical conclusion to declare all FU lifetimes safe.

**Correction.** This was first recorded as a reproduced defect. A follow-up survey of
every writeback source shows the stimulus scenario 18 injects is **not produced by any
FU in the current OoO configuration**, so it is a latent robustness gap in the dispatch
contract rather than an end-to-end defect. The reproduction below is still valid and
useful; its interpretation is what changed.

Why it cannot occur today:

| Writeback source | Behaviour for flushed/cancelled work |
|---|---|
| `load_unit` | Per-slot `ldbuf_flushed_q` is **sticky**, set for every slot on `flush_i` and for TID-matching slots on `cancelled_mask_i`; `valid_o` requires `!ldbuf_flushed_q`. A slot is freed **only** when its response actually returns (`ldbuf_r`), so a flushed load's slot cannot be reallocated while outstanding. |
| `mult` / `serdiv` | `mul_valid_op`/`div_valid_op` are gated by `~flush_i`, and `serdiv` takes `flush_i` directly, disabling its register enables. |
| ALU / branch / CSR | No multi-cycle in-flight state to return late. |
| CVXIF | `CvxifEn = 0` in `g6lc64_ooo_config_pkg`, so no external late-result source. |

The load unit therefore already implements, one level down, the discipline of "do not
reuse the resource while a result may be outstanding" — which was repair option 2. That
makes option 2 redundant at dispatch and option 1 (a generation tag on every FU
writeback) unjustified for a hazard nothing can currently produce.

**The real finding is the hidden coupling.** `g6lc_ooo_dispatch` qualifies writebacks
only with `!cancelled_mask_i[wb_id_i[w]]`, which is indexed by `trans_id` and released
when the id is dropped. Dispatch therefore cannot defend itself, and depends on an
invariant it neither states nor checks:

> **No functional unit may present a writeback for a trans_id whose instruction was
> flushed or cancelled.** Enforcement lives in each FU, and must use state that
> survives the release of `cancelled_mask_i` — sticky per-slot flags, as the load
> buffer does — because the mask is cleared as soon as the id is reallocated.

This matters for what comes next: a new writeback source (CVXIF, an accelerator, or
Phase 4 mixed residency with per-hart cancellation) that does not uphold it would make
the hazard live, and dispatch would not detect it. Note also that dispatch structurally
*cannot* detect the reuse case — once the id belongs to a live, issued instruction, a
per-TID "awaiting writeback" flag is set for the new owner too, so only an end-to-end
generation tag could distinguish. That is the cost of relaxing the invariant, and the
reason to keep it.

#### The reproduction itself (scenario 18)

`tb_g6lc_review_dispatch` scenario 18 (`REVIEW_RTL_TIDREUSE=1`) closes the gap that
scenarios 11-14 could not reach. Those rely on `cancelled_mask_i`, but the only
writeback qualification in `g6lc_ooo_dispatch` is

```
wb_value_valid[w] = wb_valid_i[w] && !wb_exc_i[w] && !cancelled_mask_i[wb_id_i[w]];
```

which is indexed by `trans_id`. Once a cancelled instruction's id is dropped and handed
to a live instruction, its cancel bit is released and a late result for the OLD owner is
indistinguishable from the NEW owner's completion.

The scenario cancels a victim holding id 2, retires the branch, drops the victim, gives
id 2 to a new instruction, adds a consumer of that instruction, and only then delivers
the victim's result. Measured on `ooo-tidreuse-confirmed-20260919`:

**the stale value 0xDEAD woke the new owner's consumer and was supplied as its operand.**

It is worse than a spurious wake: the dropped victim's physical register is correctly
freed and reallocated to the new owner (scenario 16 proves that free is right), so the
stale result lands on the live owner's own register. An early setup assertion asserting
those registers must differ was wrong and was removed — sharing them is correct.

Scenario 18 is kept as a **contract probe**: it documents precisely what dispatch would
do if the upstream invariant were broken, and it is the regression that would catch a
future FU which stops suppressing flushed results. It is recorded as an expected
failure, not as a passing gate, because no repair is warranted at dispatch.

`OoOEn=0` remains the default, and no configured target enables the affected path.

#### Cancelled retirement follow-up (Phase2)

Acknowledging a cancelled slot previously updated the architectural map, freed its
old physical register and released a checkpoint using a stale tag. The live-port
cases reproduce all three effects. `commit_arch` now qualifies those side effects
and LSQ store commit with the existing cancellation mask, leaving ROB retirement
unfiltered so dropped slots drain. Single-lane and paired older-live/younger-dropped
retirement pass six positives/six negatives; six independently restored map/free/
checkpoint faults fail. Artifacts: ooo-drop-* and ooo-drop-dual-*-20260919.

Final generic live-port fixture:86,824 cells,6,250 state bits, zero latches/SCCs
(`ooo-drop-area-20260919`), versus88,501 before this guard. No mapped PPA/IPC claim.
The SMT2 regate executable again matches c421aedc... exactly; no firmware rerun is
claimed. The collected broad gate now has11 formal task passes; fetch-IQ remains
failed/unresolved after witness/cover interruption. Default lint/synthesis pass
with prior warning counts and standalone-slang skips.

This closes the reproduced component-level drop side effects, not simultaneous
full-core trap/flush/commit or late TID reuse. The external plan's Phases0–9 preserve
that distinction and place dual-runnable service measurement before scheduler tuning.

#### Promotion gates and efficiency order

| Gate | Evidence required before promotion |
|---|---|
| Single-hart integer data/commit | Actual CSR/AMO late results must reach PRF/wakeup, not early commit-ready placeholders. The in-order AMO fix does not provide that OoO path. Preserve the cancelled commit/drop component controls; extend them to full-core trap/flush/commit. **Late WB after TID reuse: the pre-grant load path violated this invariant in live firmware and now has a directed/bounded repair. Scenario18 remains a dispatch contract probe; all other FU sources and mixed-residency lifetimes still need qualification.** |
| Full-core recovery and memory | Ordered architectural retirement against an independent reference; precise traps; branch/exception/commit collisions; LSQ credits, byte forwarding, fences and LR/SC. A fixture PASS cannot replace this. |
| SMT ownership | Hart-indexed architectural/speculative maps, physical-register ownership and checkpoint identity; hart-tagged IQ/ROB/LSQ and per-hart cancellation. Start with the existing drained handoff; do not assume it preserves an unbanked committed PRF. |
| FP class | Separate integer/FP identities through rename, readiness, PRF, writeback and commit; cross-class alias and exception controls. Keep the FP guard until integrated qualification. |
| Performance/area | On functionally qualified configurations, sweep one queue/width/policy at a time. Measure useful committed work per cycle, stalls, fairness, generic/mapped area and timing. Report geomean speedup and worst-hart slowdown, not just aggregate retirement. |

Prefer shared control and result qualification before widening queues or duplicating
banks. Do not add a new PRF write port for late results without proving an existing
port cannot safely be reused; do not reuse one without an exclusivity proof. A
memory-dependence predictor may relax the conservative age gate only with alias
validation and replay. Compiler wall-time gains are not hardware IPC gains.

### Continuation review: lifetime closure remains open

#### Checkpoint retirement (repaired, leaf-verified)

Rename checkpoints were allocated per branch and released only by mispredict or
flush, so the capacity gate stalled dispatch permanently after `CKPT_DEPTH`
correctly predicted branches. Checkpoints are taken in program order and commit is
in program order, so the pool is now a ring: allocate at the tail, retire at the
head on commit. A checkpoint level is a ring slot, and its age is its distance from
the head, so an older branch still unwinds to its own level and consumes every
younger one; a level outside the live window falls back to the youngest. Retirement
is bounded by what is live, so a stale strobe cannot advance the head past the tail.

`g6lc_ooo_dispatch` derives the retire strobes from `commit_ack_i` and the per-tid
checkpoint tag, writes that tag unconditionally at dispatch so a reused slot cannot
retire a checkpoint it never took, and clears the resolving branch's tag on
mispredict because the unwind already consumed it.

`review-rename-ckpt-release-v1` passes 18 records (9 positives, 9 injected-oracle
negatives), including reuse of the retired slot and preserved recovery after a
retirement. `review-rename-ckpt-fault-v1` disables retirement and the new scenario
fails as contracted, so it is the discriminator for the original defect.
`review-dispatch-ckpt-release-v1` passes 14 records with the new wiring. This is
leaf and dispatch-fixture evidence: it does not close full OoO, and the hart/FP
legality restrictions stay.

#### LSQ group credits (repaired, leaf-verified)

Admission asked only whether *any* load/store entry was free, but dispatch is
all-or-nothing across the group, and a surplus allocation is silently discarded by
the queue's placement loop — leaving that memory op live in the ROB/IQ with no queue
entry to order or forward it. `g6lc_lsq` now exports free-entry counts and dispatch
compares them against the group's memory-op count. The counts come from registered
state and the group size from ungated intent, so admission still cannot feed back
through `can_go`.

`review-lsq-credit-dispatch-v2` passes 16 records and `review-lsq-credit-lsq-v1`
passes 24; `review-lsq-credit-fault-v2` restores the any-free-entry term and the new
group-credit scenario fails as contracted. Scope: one geometry
(`LsqLoadEntries=LsqStoreEntries=4`, two issue ports) at fixture level.

#### Phase 4 step 1 landed: per-hart rename namespaces (2026-09-19)

`g6lc_rename` now carries `NR_HARTS` (default 1) with per-hart architectural state,
while the physical pool stays shared:

* `map_q` / `amap_q` are `[hart][arch]`; `hart_i` accompanies each dispatch port and
  `commit_hart_i` each commit port.
* `free_q` / `busy_q` are **unchanged and shared** — the shared pool is what makes SMT
  cheaper than two cores, and busy is a property of a physical register.
* Checkpoints record their owning hart (`ckpt_hart_q`), so recovery restores only that
  hart's map.
* **Per-hart checkpoint rings.** `ckpt_head_q`/`ckpt_cnt_q` are now one ring per hart
  over a static slice of `CKPT_DEPTH` (`CKPT_PER_HART = CKPT_DEPTH / NH`). A single
  shared ring cannot support per-hart flush or independent recovery: removing one
  hart's checkpoints would punch a hole in the middle, which a head/count pair cannot
  represent. The static split is also the partition-before-elastic-policy rule applied
  to speculation depth. Capacity, allocation, retirement and unwind are all per hart,
  and the resolving hart is derived from `ckpt_hart_q[level]` rather than needing a new
  mispredict-hart input. Note `mispredict_level_i` is an absolute slot while
  head/count are slice-relative, so the unwind converts before comparing.
* A checkpoint's `ckpt_alloc` set is now only marked for allocations **of the same
  hart**. Without that, a peer hart's allocation made after this branch would be
  squashed by this branch's mispredict — the aliasing the namespaces exist to prevent,
  reintroduced through the recovery path rather than the map.
* `x0` never consults the map, so physical 0 remains the shared zero for every hart and
  costs nothing per hart. Reset gives hart *h* physicals `31*h + 1 .. 31*h + 31` and
  starts the pool at `31*NH + 1`.

With `NR_HARTS=1` the reset bindings are exactly the previous ones (`map[0][i] = i`,
pool from 32), so a single-hart build is unchanged — confirmed by `g6lc64_smt2`
rebuilding **byte-identical** (`ba1f8f6a722829aa…`).

Verification: rename, dispatch, drop and late-result fixtures all pass; the bounded
rename formal proof passes and its checker-negative still produces a counterexample
(the `map_q[0]` mutation site was re-pointed to `map_q[0][0]`, since the index now
means hart 0's x0 rather than the whole map); `g6lc64_ooo_int` lint 11 warnings at
baseline and synthesis clean.

**Physical-register ownership and per-hart flush.** `owner_q[phys]` records which hart
each physical belongs to, set at allocation and seeded to match the reset bindings. It
is what makes `flush_hart_i[h]` possible: the shared freelist records no owner, so
without it a hart park/reset/trap could not tell which registers to reclaim. A per-hart
flush restores that hart's map from its own architectural map, returns every physical
it owns that its committed map no longer references, and clears its checkpoints —
leaving the peer's allocations and speculation live. Ownership is stale on a *free*
register, which is harmless: allocation overwrites it, and the flush only reclaims
non-free entries.

**Tested at NrHarts=2 — without relaxing the core guard.** `g6lc_rename` is
package-free, so `tb_g6lc_review_rename` instantiates it directly at `NR_HARTS=2`
(`REVIEW_RTL_RENAME_SMT=1`, `-GNR_HARTS=2 -GPRF_ENTRIES=80 -GPRF_W=7`; the wider PRF is
required because 31 committed physicals per hart no longer fit in 40). Three of the six
planned Phase 4 tests now run for real, each with a failing control:

| Scenario | Property | Negative control |
|---|---|---|
| 11 | namespace isolation — each hart reads back its own `x5` | `RENAME_HART_ALIAS` |
| 12 | independent recovery — hart 1's mispredict spares hart 0 | `RENAME_PEER_SQUASHED` |
| 13 | per-hart flush — hart 1 reset, hart 0's rename untouched | `RENAME_HART_FLUSH_SPILL` |

All six cases match. This is the first genuine `NrHarts>1` evidence in the OoO path;
the remaining three tests (no double allocation, per-hart commit, per-hart late-WB)
need the IQ/ROB/LSQ hart tagging that has not landed.

**The guard is untouched.** `check_cfg` still refuses `OoOEn && NrHarts > 1`, and the
fixture negative still fails to build as required. This is the namespace groundwork
only — the remaining Phase 4 items below (IQ/ROB/LSQ hart tagging, per-hart
cancellation, and above all physical-register ownership for per-hart flush) must land
before the guard can move.

#### Phase 4 scope and verification plan for NrHarts>1 (2026-09-19)

The guard `assert(!(Cfg.OoOEn && Cfg.NrHarts > 1))` still refuses the combination, and
the fixture negative confirms it after every change in this pass
(`REVIEW_RTL_ILLEGAL=smt`, `-GHARTS=2`, must fail to build — it does). A per-hart test
cannot be written before the interface exists, because `g6lc_rename` has no hart signal
to address, so this is the specification that test work has to implement against.

State in `g6lc_rename` today, and what each must become:

| State | Now | For NrHarts>1 |
|---|---|---|
| `map_q[31:0]`, `amap_q[31:0]` | one namespace | **per hart** — this is the aliasing |
| `free_q[PRF_ENTRIES]` | shared | **stays shared**; a shared physical pool is the point, and sharing it is what makes SMT cheaper than two cores |
| `busy_q[PRF_ENTRIES]` | shared | **stays shared** — busy is a property of a physical register, not of a hart |
| `ckpt_map_q[CKPT_DEPTH][32]` | one namespace | per hart, or hart-tagged, so a hart-1 mispredict cannot roll back hart 0 |
| `rs1_i/rs2_i/rd_i`, `commit_rd_i` | 5-bit arch reg | need an accompanying hart id |

**The hard part is not the maps, it is per-hart flush.** Because the freelist is
shared, nothing records which physical registers belong to which hart, so parking or
resetting one hart cannot reclaim its registers without new ownership state. Resist
splitting the freelist to dodge this: that would give up the shared-pool utilisation
that justifies SMT in the first place.

Tests Phase 4 must satisfy, each with a failing control:

1. **Namespace isolation** — hart 0 and hart 1 each write their own `x5` and read back
   their own value. Fails today by construction; this is the headline gate.
2. **Independent recovery** — a hart-1 mispredict leaves hart 0's map and in-flight
   allocations untouched.
3. **No double allocation** — concurrent allocation from the shared freelist never
   hands the same physical register to both harts.
4. **Per-hart commit** — committing hart 0's `x5` updates only hart 0's architectural
   map.
5. **Per-hart flush** — flushing hart 1 reclaims exactly hart 1's physicals and leaves
   hart 0's allocations live. This is the one that needs the ownership state above.
6. **Per-hart late-writeback invariant** — carried over from the TID-reuse finding: a
   cancelled hart-1 result must never complete, wake or supply data for hart 0. Each
   FU's suppression must hold per hart, not just per core.

#### Phase 5 step 1 landed: split FP register class in rename (2026-09-19)

`g6lc_rename` now carries a second, **separate** register class behind
`FPRF_ENTRIES`/`FPRF_W` (0 = disabled, bit-identical to before):

* `fmap_q` / `famap_q` per hart, `ffree_q` / `fbusy_q` as their own pool and busy
  table, and `fprs1_o` / `fprs2_o` / `fprd_o` / `fprd_old_o` on their own ports — the
  two classes index different files, so overloading one tag would force the consumer
  to re-derive the class to know which file to read.
* Separate writeback (`fwb_*`), free (`ffree_*`) and commit (`commit_is_fpr_i`,
  `commit_fprd_i`) paths.
* **Checkpoints stay shared.** Splitting the *files* does not mean splitting the
  *control*: a branch is one program-order event, so one checkpoint snapshots both
  maps and recovery restores both. `ckpt_falloc` mirrors `ckpt_alloc` per pool.

The asymmetry that drove the split shows up everywhere in the diff, which is the
clearest argument it was the right call: the integer path guards `rd != 0` and
`prd != 0` and reserves physical 0, while **every FP path deliberately has no such
guard** — `f0` is an ordinary writable register, its allocation pool starts at 0, its
writeback clears busy for physical 0, and its committed map covers all 32 entries.
Threading that exception through shared logic is exactly what a unified file would
have required.

Capacity is checked per pool, so an FP-heavy group cannot stall out integer
allocation — a benefit a shared freelist would not have given.

**`rs3` renaming and the class-confusion guard.** Rename now takes `rs3_i` /
`is_fpr_rs3_i` and emits `fprs3_o` / `rs3_ready_o`. The caller supplies `rs3_i` from
`result[4:0]`, since there is no rs3 field (see the constraint note below).

Adding a second class made an existing line unsafe, which is worth naming because it
is the whole hazard of two namespaces in miniature: the intra-group dependency bypass
matched `rs1_i[p] == rd_i[e]` on the architectural *number* alone. `x5` and `f5` share
a number but are different registers, so once FP exists an integer producer could
satisfy an FP consumer. `do_alloc` / `do_falloc` already separate the **producers**;
the consumer side now carries `!is_fpr_rsN` (and the FP bypass matches on
`do_falloc`), so the two classes cannot cross. The FP bypass also omits the `rd != 0`
filter the integer path needs — an `f0` producer must be visible to an `f0` consumer.

**The FP class is tested at `FPRF_ENTRIES>0`, not just proven inert when off.** Like
the per-hart work, `g6lc_rename` is package-free, so `tb_g6lc_review_rename` runs it
with a real FP file (`REVIEW_RTL_RENAME_FP=1`, `-GFPRF_ENTRIES=64 -GFPRF_W=7`) without
relaxing `!(OoOEn && FpPresent)`. Each scenario targets a property of the new code and
has a failing control:

| Scenario | Property | Negative control |
|---|---|---|
| 20 | `x5` and `f5` are different registers — no cross-class satisfaction | `RENAME_FP_CLASS` |
| 21 | `f0` is renamed, tracked busy and read back, unlike `x0` | `RENAME_FP_F0` |
| 22 | a branch checkpoints **both** maps, so FP recovery restores the pre-branch mapping | `RENAME_FP_RECOVER` |
| 23 | `rs3` renames from the FP map, and FP writeback clears busy for FP physical 0 | `RENAME_FP_RS3` |

All 8 cases match. Writing these before the dispatch-side FP PRF was deliberate: the
class added ~370 lines whose only prior evidence was "FP-off is unchanged", and
building the PRF on top of untested rename logic would have compounded any error in it.

Verification: rename, dual-hart (NR_HARTS=2) and dispatch fixtures pass; the rename
formal proof passes with its checker-negative still failing; `g6lc64_smt2` rebuilds
**byte-identical** (`ba1f8f6a722829aa…`).

**Step 3 resolved to "not needed", which is the better answer.** The FP PRF has no
commit-write mirror, and that is a conclusion rather than an omission. The integer
mirror exists only because `commit_stage` *substitutes* a value at commit —
`csr_rdata_i` for CSR, `amo_resp_i.result` for AMO — that never reaches `wb_data_i`.
Neither CSR nor AMO has an FP destination (`is_rd_fpr` excludes both), and for an FP
destination `commit_stage` writes plain `commit_instr.result`, i.e. exactly the value
the execute writeback already put in the FP PRF. So **no FP value is produced at
commit**, and no FP write port is required — which matters, because PRF ports are the
measured area driver.

The property that makes this sound is that the two classes are mutually exclusive at
commit, enforced by an `if/else` on `is_rd_fpr` in `commit_stage`. That is now pinned
by a `translate_off` assertion in dispatch (`OOO_COMMIT_CLASS`): if it ever broke, the
integer mirror would silently write the integer PRF for an FP-destination instruction.
The reasoning depends on someone else's if/else, so it gets a tripwire rather than a
comment.

**Step 4, both halves, also resolved without new datapath.**

*fflags ordering is correct by construction.* Flags are not applied at execute: they
ride in the scoreboard entry as `ex.cause[4:0]` and `commit_stage` writes them to the
CSR at **commit**, ORing port 0 and port 1 in program order for a dual commit. OoO
execution does not reorder commit — the ROB retires in order — so the accumulation
point is already correctly ordered and needs nothing from Phase 5.

*`FLen > XLEN` is unreachable, and is now stated rather than left latent.* The OoO FP
writeback narrows the XLEN-wide bus (`fprf_wdata = wb_data_i[FLen-1:0]`), so an FP
result wider than the integer datapath cannot be delivered. RV32+D is legal RISC-V and
would hit it, but no configured target does — every RV32 package has `RVD=0`. Note the
**in-order path contains the identical construct** (`fp_wdata_pack =
wdata_i[FLen-1:0]`), so this limit is pre-existing, not introduced here. Rather than
invent a 64-bit-result-on-a-32-bit-bus mechanism for a configuration nobody builds,
`check_cfg` now refuses the combination for the OoO path:

```
assert (!(Cfg.OoOEn && Cfg.FpPresent && Cfg.FLen > Cfg.XLEN));
```

Widening the writeback bus is the fix if RV32+D is ever wanted. All existing targets
lint at their recorded baselines with the assertion in place, so it excludes nothing
that exists.

#### Measured: the FP-enabled full core elaborates and synthesizes (2026-09-19)

Rather than relax the guard on module-level evidence, the guard was **temporarily
disabled locally, the measurement taken, and both sites restored** — verified afterward
by `g6lc64_ooo` being refused again. Nothing about the shipped legality contract
changed; this is evidence gathered to inform the decision, not the decision.

| Gate on `g6lc64_ooo` (RVF=RVD=1, OoOEn=1) | Result |
|---|---|
| lint | **10 warnings, no guard fired** |
| synth `check -assert` | **clean, 32 warnings** |

**This is the first time `g6lc64_ooo` has ever elaborated.** Until Phase 5 it could not
get past `check_cfg`, so it had never been linted or synthesized at all. The FP split
register class, the FP PRF, the `rs3`/`op_c` path and the widened scoreboard entry all
hold together at full-core scale, and introduce no combinational loop.

**A second guard was found in the process.** The refusal is enforced in *two* places:
`check_cfg` and an elaboration `$error` inside `g6lc_ooo_dispatch` itself. Only the
first was known when Phase 5 was scoped. The dispatch guard's message is now
**factually stale** — it says "FP destinations are not renamed or tracked", and they
now are. Both must be updated together, and the note above them ("Remove these only
together with per-hart namespaces / an FP register class") is the reason they were
found rather than silently bypassed.

**The justification for keeping the guard has changed, even though the guard stays.**
It is no longer "there is no FP register class". It is now "the FP register class
exists, elaborates and synthesizes, but has never been simulated, booted, or compared
against an independent reference". That is a weaker reason — and a much more specific
one, which is what the next step has to close.

Both guard messages were rewritten to say that, in both copies:

| Guard | Was | Now |
|---|---|---|
| `NrHarts>1` | "no per-hart rename namespace" | "OoO IQ/ROB/LSQ are hart-blind: aliases memory ordering and forwarding" |
| `FpPresent` | "no FP register class" | "FP register class is implemented but unqualified: no FP simulation or reference comparison" |

A guard that misstates its own reason is how a stale refusal outlives the work that
removed the reason — and, worse, how the *real* remaining risk gets lost. The hart
guard is the sharper case: per-hart rename exists and is tested, but the IQ, ROB and
LSQ still contain no hart signal, so a load can be ordered against and forwarded from
the peer hart's speculative stores. That is a different and more dangerous defect than
"the rename map is shared", and the old wording no longer pointed at it.

The refusal harness matches on this text, so rewording the messages failed the
illegal-config negatives immediately rather than passing against a different guard;
the expectations were updated to `hart-blind` and `implemented but unqualified`.

#### The FP bring-up test exists (2026-09-19)

`verif/tests/custom/ooo/ooo_fp_rename.S`, registered in `testlist_ooo_l3.yaml`. It is
written to run on `OoOEn=0` **and** `OoOEn=1`, which matters: the in-order FP core is
the control that validates the test before it is used as an oracle for the OoO one.

Each block targets a property of the split class that a shared or absent FP namespace
gets wrong, rather than exercising FP generally:

| Block | Property | What a wrong implementation does |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | FP dependency chain | reads a stale physical instead of waiting on the FP busy table |
| 2 | FMA `rs3`, incl. chained onto its own result | never renames the third source, whose number rides in the imm field |
| 3 | `f0` written and read, and as an FMA `rs3` | applies the integer `rd==0 means no destination` rule and drops the write |
| 4 | `x5` and `f5` live simultaneously, interleaved | a shared map, or a bypass matching the register NUMBER without the class, crosses them |
| 5 | FP load/store round trip | misses `is_rd_fpr` on LOAD, so the load's FP destination is never renamed |
| 6 | FP WAW with a reader in between | gives both writes the same physical, so the reader sees the younger value |
| 7 | mixed FP/integer loop | the two pools allocating concurrently |

It sets `mstatus.FS` first — without that every FP instruction traps and the test would
"pass" by never executing its checks.

**Status: assembles clean; control run available on `g6lc64_smt2`.**

A first attempt at the control used `cv64a6_imafdc_sv39` and failed to build the C++
testbench. That was **my choice of target, not an infrastructure blocker** — the
correction is worth recording because the first reading was wrong in a way that would
have caused unnecessary testbench surgery:

* `Makefile` picks `TB_CPP` by target NAME: `g6lc*` targets get
  `corev_apu/tb/g6lc_tb.cpp`, everything else gets `corev_apu/tb/ariane_tb.cpp`.
* `g6lc_tb.cpp` is already config-aware. `G6LC_TB_BANKED` is defined by the build only
  when the target's config has `NrHarts>1`, and `G6LC_TB_H1(expr)` evaluates hart-1 and
  `gen_smt` reads only on banked builds, compiling to 0 otherwise — with a comment
  warning that a bare hart-1 read "silently aliases hart-0 state when non-banked".
* `ariane_tb.cpp` is the one whose SMT2/AI probes are unguarded, and
  `AGENTS-todo.md` already records exactly this: *"non-g6lc cv\* targets still can't
  use its hierarchy probes (needs same pass or -DG6LC_TB_NO_HIER)"*.

So this is a **known, documented limitation of the `cv*` path**, not a newly-found
blocker, and every `g6lc*` target — including `g6lc64_ooo_int` — already builds its
testbench correctly at `NrHarts=1`.

The control does not need a new target either: **`g6lc64_smt2` sets `RVF=1, RVD=1`**.
It is in-order (`OoOEn=0`), has full double-precision FP, and is the model whose
dual-hart boot is already validated — so the FP test can be qualified on it directly,
against a harness that is known to work.

**The test is now a qualified oracle.** Run on that model via
`verif/regress/remote/run_ooo_fp_review.py`:

| Run | Verdict |
|---|---|
| in-order FP model | `*** SUCCESS *** (tohost = 0) after 2308 cycles` |
| same, one expected constant perturbed (`FP_REVIEW_NEGATIVE=1`) | `*** FAILED *** (tohost = 1) after 1747 cycles` |

The negative control matters more than the pass. A self-checking program that traps
before its first check, or whose FP instructions never execute because `mstatus.FS`
was left clear, exits 0 just as convincingly as one that genuinely passes. The
mutation perturbs the **FMA** result specifically — a check reachable only if the FP
path really ran — and the run fails, at a different cycle count, so the checks are
live rather than decorative.

The runner also extracts `tohost` from the linked binary rather than hard-coding it,
and requires an explicit `SUCCESS` verdict: a run that merely fails to crash does not
count as a pass.

#### The FP OoO run: two defects, one of them mine (2026-09-19)

An FP+OoO model was built behind the same temporary local guard relaxation (guards
restored and verified afterwards; `g6lc64_ooo` refuses again).

**First failure — a pre-existing config defect, not FP.** The model aborted at time 0:

```
load_unit.sv:745: DcacheIdWidth parameter is not wide enough to encode pending loads
```

| Config | `DcacheIdWidth` | `NrLoadBufEntries` | Needs |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `g6lc64_smt2` | **3** | 8 | 3 ✓ |
| `g6lc64_ooo`, `g6lc64_ooo_int` | **1** | 8 | 3 ✗ |

`g6lc64_smt2` already carries the fix *and the explanation* — "G1n: hang-7 raised
NrLoadBufEntries to 8; 1-bit D$ rid truncated". **The same fix was never propagated to
the OoO packages**, and nothing caught it because those configs could never reach
simulation: the assertion is `translate_off`, so lint and synthesis pass regardless.
Both are now set to 3. This is precisely the class of defect that building a runnable
baseline exists to find.

**Second failure — my own oracle was broken, and the negative control caught it.**
After the fix, both the positive *and* the negative run reported
`*** SUCCESS *** (tohost = 0) after 2000000 cycles`. That number is the cycle **cap**:
on timeout the driver still prints SUCCESS, so a hang is textually indistinguishable
from a pass. The negative control was supposed to fail and did not, which is the only
reason this surfaced rather than being reported as "FP works on OoO".

`run_ooo_fp_review.py` now requires the retired cycle count to be strictly below the
cap. With that, the two runs separate properly:

| Model | Result |
|---|---|
| in-order FP (`g6lc64_smt2`) | **PASS**, 2,308 cycles |
| FP + OoO (`g6lc64_ooo`) | **TIMEOUT at 2,000,000 cycles — does not complete** |

**So FP on the OoO path does not work yet** — and the cause is now located.

Two discriminating runs narrowed it without guesswork:

| Test | Model | Result |
|---|---|---|
| integer (`ooo_ilp_chain`) | integer OoO (`g6lc64_ooo_int`) | **PASS**, 1,109 cycles |
| integer (`ooo_ilp_chain`) | **FP-enabled** OoO (`g6lc64_ooo`) | **PASS**, 1,172 cycles |
| FP (`ooo_fp_rename`) | FP-enabled OoO | **TIMEOUT** |

The second row is the important one: the FP-enabled core boots and retires integer
code perfectly well, so neither FP *presence* nor the OoO path is broken. The hang is
triggered by FP *instructions*. (An earlier reading blamed a hang at `npc=0x10040`;
that was a misread — those `[boot]` lines are a capped early-cycle trace, not the
stall point.)

**Root cause — a gap in this Phase-5 work.** `g6lc_iq` is wired
`.wb_valid_i(wb_value_valid)`, and step 2 redefined that signal as *integer
completions only*:

```
wb_value_valid[w]  = wb_valid_i[w] && … && !tid_is_fpr_q[wb_id_i[w]];
fwb_value_valid[w] = … &&  tid_is_fpr_q[wb_id_i[w]];
```

The FP completion was routed to rename's FP busy table (`fwb_valid_i`) but **never to
the IQ's wakeup**. So an IQ entry waiting on an FP producer is never woken, never
issues, and the ROB head never retires — a deadlock, which is precisely the observed
timeout.

**Fix applied — the deadlock is cleared.** `g6lc_iq` now takes `FPRF_W`,
`fwb_valid_i`/`fwb_prd_i`, per-entry FP source tags (`fprs1/2/3`) and per-source class
bits (`fpr_rs1/2/3`), plus an `rs3_rdy` term for the FP third source. Each source now
wakes from its own class: integer sources on `wb_prd_i` with the `!= 0`
no-destination guard, FP sources on `fwb_prd_i` **without** it, since FP physical 0 is
a real destination. The same split is applied to the same-cycle dispatch capture, so a
newly dispatched waiter cannot miss an FP writeback pulse.

| Model | Before the IQ fix | After |
|---|---|---|
| FP + OoO | **TIMEOUT** at 2,000,000 cycles | **completes in ~920 cycles** |

**A second, separate defect is now exposed underneath it.** The program completes but
fails its first check. The test was extended to report the failing block as its exit
code, which names it precisely:

| Model | Verdict |
|---|---|
| in-order FP (`g6lc64_smt2`) | `SUCCESS` after 2,420 cycles |
| FP + OoO (`g6lc64_ooo`) | `FAILED (tohost = 1)` after 920 cycles — **block 1** |

Block 1 is the FP dependency chain — a straight `fadd.d` sequence, the simplest FP
rename-and-wakeup case there is. The same tagged binary passes in-order, so the test
and its expected constants are sound and the fault is in the OoO FP path.

**Block 1 diagnosed and fixed: FP operands were read from the wrong file.** Dispatch
assembled `op_a`/`op_b` from the **integer** PRF only, and `issue_read_operands`
carried an explicit `!(FpPresent && is_rs1_fpr(...))` exclusion that sent every FP
source to the **architectural** f-register file. That file by construction holds no
in-flight renamed value, so a dependent `fadd.d` read a stale operand — exactly the
block-1 symptom. Step 2 had wired the FP file's third read port to `op_c` and left
rs1/rs2 on the integer path.

The fix delivers FP sources from the FP physical file on the same `op_a`/`op_b` ports
(with FP writeback bypass, and the same for `op_c`), and drops the exclusion so the
renamed value is preferred for *both* classes.

It also needed a structural decision: the FP file has **three read ports for the whole
group**, so at most one issue port can read FP operands per cycle. A second FP
consumer in the same group is now **held back** rather than issued with operands it
cannot read. That gate is applied to the IQ's *raw* valid, not to `issue_valid_o` —
`fp_port_sel` is derived from the raw valid, so gating the output would have closed a
combinational loop through the very signal being computed.

**Result: the FP+OoO core now completes the program instead of hanging.** A claim made
earlier in this work — that "block 1 now passes" — was **withdrawn**; see the
attribution defect below. What is established is the timeout clearing, not any
particular block's correctness.

**A third defect remains, and it is nondeterministic.** `run_ooo_fp_review.py` gained
a `FP_REVIEW_STOP_AFTER=N` bisect that truncates the program after block N — necessary
because a hang produces no exit code, so unlike a wrong result it cannot name its own
block. The sweep does not converge on a single block:

| stop after | verdict |
|---|---|
| 2 | SUCCESS |
| 3 | **FAILED, block 3** |
| 4 | SUCCESS |
| 5 | **FAILED, exit 1337** |
| 6 | **FAILED, block 4** |

Blocks 1–2 pass consistently, including the chained FMA whose `rs3` is an in-flight
renamed producer. Beyond that the *same* block passes or fails depending only on what
code follows it, which is the signature of a scheduling-dependent race rather than a
deterministic logic error. Exit **1337** is the sharpest datum: that is not a block id,
so `s11` — an **integer** register — was corrupted. Whatever is wrong is not confined
to the FP class; it leaks into integer state.

**My own instrumentation was the first thing found wrong.** The block id was carried
in `s11` and read at the exit point — in a test whose whole purpose is to hunt
register corruption. Exit **1337** proved `s11` itself was corruptible, which makes
every block attribution above suspect. The test now materialises the id **at** each
failure site (`fail1:` … `fail7:`, `li a0, N`) so nothing carries it through the code
under test. Re-running the sweep with trustworthy ids gives a *different* and still
inconsistent picture:

| stop after | verdict (reliable) | previously reported |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | FAILED block 2 | SUCCESS |
| 3 | FAILED **block 1** | FAILED block 3 |
| 4 | FAILED block 2 | SUCCESS |
| 5 | **SUCCESS** | FAILED, exit 1337 |
| 6 | **SUCCESS** | FAILED block 4 |

So even block 1 fails under one truncation while the two longest variants pass
outright. **The earlier "block 1 now passes" conclusion is withdrawn** — it rested on
the corrupted carrier. The corrected test still passes in-order (`SUCCESS`, 2,322
cycles), so the oracle remains sound; only the OoO attribution was wrong.

Three hypotheses have now been eliminated rather than assumed:

1. *An FP destination also consumes an integer physical.* Disproved by reading
   `g6lc_rename`: allocation is mutually exclusive (`if (FP…) … else if (…)`).
2. *`tid_is_fpr_q` goes stale, so an integer op is treated as FP at commit* — which
   would skip its map update and its predecessor free, explaining integer corruption.
   Disproved by a directed assertion (`OOO_COMMIT_CLASS_STALE`) comparing the tracked
   class against the committing opcode: it **never fires**.
3. *FP physical 0 / `f0` is mishandled.* Disproved in software: rewriting block 3's
   `f0` to `f20` reproduces the failure **bit for bit** — same verdict, same 1,010
   cycles.

#### The cause is NOT FP: the OoO memory path hangs on its own (2026-09-19)

Before reaching for a waveform, one discriminator settled it — run an existing
**integer** test that exercises memory on every model:

| Test | in-order | integer-only OoO (`g6lc64_ooo_int`) | FP-enabled OoO |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ooo_ilp_chain` (pure ALU) | PASS 2,315 cy | **PASS 1,109 cy** | PASS 1,172 cy |
| `ooo_mem_dep` (loads/stores) | PASS 2,168 cy | **TIMEOUT at 400,000** | **TIMEOUT at 400,000** |

`ooo_mem_dep` hangs on the **integer-only** OoO configuration, with no FP anywhere in
the design or the program. So the defect I have been chasing through the FP test is
**pre-existing in the OoO memory path**, not something Phase 5 introduced. It had
never been observed because this core had never executed a program until today —
`ooo_ilp_chain` is pure ALU work and passes, which is exactly why the first OoO
execution looked healthy.

This also explains the nondeterminism. The FP test is full of memory traffic (`la`,
`fsd`, `fld`, and the surrounding runtime), so truncating it at different points
changes the load/store pattern — and with it whether the memory-path defect is hit.
The "same block passes or fails depending on what follows" behaviour is what a
memory-ordering stall looks like through a test that was never designed to isolate one.

**Consequences for the Phase 5 conclusions:**

* The two FP fixes stand on their own evidence: the IQ FP wakeup turned a hard hang
  into a completing run, and the FP operand path was demonstrably reading the
  architectural f-register file. Both were real defects in code I wrote.
* No FP *correctness* verdict can be drawn from the current FP test runs at all, in
  either direction, while the memory path is broken underneath them.
* The right next target is `ooo_mem_dep` on `g6lc64_ooo_int`: an **FP-free,
  deterministic, reproducible** hang on a configuration that is legal today and needs
  no guard relaxation to build or run. That is a far better debugging position than
  the FP test, and it must be fixed before FP bring-up resumes.

##### Minimal reproductions: two distinct OoO memory defects

`verif/tests/custom/ooo/ooo_mem_min.S` adds one construct per stage
(`-DSTAGE=n`), so the first failing stage names the mechanism instead of leaving a
whole test to bisect. On `g6lc64_ooo_int`:

| Stage | Construct | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | store only, never read back | PASS 865 cy |
| 2 | store, then load a different line | PASS 860 cy |
| 3 | store, then load the **same address** | PASS 866 cy |
| 4 | two stores, then load the **older** one | **FAILED — wrong data**, 891 cy |
| 5 | store/load inside a loop | **HANG** at 200,000 |

Stages 1–3 passing is as informative as stage 4 failing: stores retire, loads
complete, and same-address store-to-load forwarding is correct. The defect needs **two
live stores**.

Stage 5 is a separate failure — repeated LSQ allocate/free deadlocks — and may or may
not share a cause with stage 4. They are recorded as two defects until evidence links
them.

**One hypothesis already disproved.** Stage 4 stores to `8(sp)` and `16(sp)`, which
share a cache line, suggesting the forward might match at line granularity. It does
not: the LSQ hazard query compares with `same_word(...)` plus a byte-lane overlap test
(`g6lc_lsq.sv`), so two different words cannot collide there. The same code documents
that it is only the *hazard* decision — "the LSU `store_buffer` owns the byte-exact
data forward (full PA + be, both queues, commit handoff)" — so the data path is the
next place to look, not the queue.

**Characterised to one cycle.** Stage 4 compared two registers, so either could have
been at fault. Splitting the check and varying only the gap between the load and its
consumer isolates it exactly:

| Stage | Shape | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 4 | two stores, `ld`, consumer **immediately** after | **FAILS** |
| 10 | **one** store, `ld`, consumer immediately after | PASS |
| 8 | two stores, `ld`, **one `nop`**, consumer | PASS |
| 9 | two stores, `ld`, two `nop`s, consumer | PASS |
| 6/7 | two stores, `ld`, a multi-instruction `li`, consumer | PASS |

So the fault needs **both** conditions — a second live store **and** a zero-cycle gap
before the consumer — and **a single independent instruction is enough to hide it**.
That is a one-cycle early-wakeup signature: with two stores live, a load's result is
made available to its consumer exactly one cycle before it is correct.

This also explains why stages 6 and 7 passed and nearly sent me the wrong way: their
`li` of a 64-bit immediate expands to several instructions, which silently supplied
the gap. The apparent "proximity of the two stores doesn't matter" conclusion from
stage 7 is therefore not evidence either way — the gap, not the addressing, was doing
the work.

**Mechanism, read from source.** The LSQ computes a precise data-availability gate —
`stl_stall_o`, which asserts when a matching older store's data is not yet present
(`!data_v`) or an older store's address is still unresolved. `g6lc_ooo_dispatch`
computes `mem_stall = md_stall || stl_stall` … and then passes **`.mem_stall_i(1'b0)`**
to the IQ. The precise gate is calculated and discarded.

That is deliberate and documented: feeding it back would close a combinational loop,
because `stl_stall` derives from `ld_qaddr`, which derives from `issue_valid_o`, which
is what the gate would suppress. The comment argues the IQ's own per-entry age gate
covers it — a load is held while any store **older** than it is live in
`st_live_mask_i`.

The measurement says that substitution is not equivalent. The age gate is a *coarse
ordering* proxy: it clears as soon as the older store leaves the live mask, whereas
`stl_stall` tracks when the store's **data** is actually visible to the load path. With
one store in flight those edges coincide closely enough; with two draining, the mask
clears one cycle before the `store_buffer` handoff makes the data visible, and a load
whose consumer is immediately adjacent reads the stale value. That is exactly the
"needs two live stores **and** a zero-cycle gap, one nop hides it" signature.

So the fix is not to re-enable `stl_stall` at select — that is the loop the comment
correctly refuses. It has to act **after** select: either suppress the load's
completion/writeback when `stl_stall` says its data was not yet available and let it
re-issue, or register the gate and hold the consumer's wakeup by a cycle. Both are
real design work with a timing cost, and both need this five-instruction reproduction
as their gate.

**Tested, and REFUTED.** The hypothesis predicted that making the age gate one cycle
more conservative would fix stage 4, so each store was held "live" for an extra cycle
(`st_live_mask | st_live_mask_q`, loads only, so it cannot stall the store drain it is
waiting on). The result was worse, not better:

| Stage | Baseline | With the one-cycle-longer mask |
|---|---|---|
| 3 (one store, same-address load) | PASS 866 | **FAILED** `tohost=0x40002000` |
| 4 (two stores) | FAILED | **FAILED** `0x40002000` |
| 10 (one store, adjacent consumer) | PASS 866 | **FAILED** `0x40002000` |
| 5 (loop) | HANG | HANG |

Two previously-passing stages broke, and the exit value is not a program exit code at
all, so the intervention introduced a *different* fault rather than exposing the
original one. The change was reverted and the baseline re-measured to confirm it is
exactly restored (S3 PASS 866, S4 FAILED, S10 PASS 866).

So the "age gate clears one cycle early" story does **not** survive contact with the
design: something else consumes that mask timing, or the load's correctness does not
hinge on the mask edge at all. **This is the fourth hypothesis this investigation has
eliminated by test rather than by argument** — along with the FP-physical double
allocation, the stale `tid_is_fpr_q`, and `f0`/physical-0 handling.

That pattern is itself the conclusion worth acting on: the OoO memory path is not
yielding to source-level reasoning, and the next step should be **waveform evidence**
from the five-instruction stage-4 reproduction — the actual cycle at which the load's
data is sampled versus when the store's data becomes visible — rather than a fifth
guess.

##### Traced: the protection window ends at commit, but visibility lags

A temporary `$display` probe in `g6lc_lsq` (since removed — the testharness whitelists
plusargs, so it could not be gated) recorded what the queue actually decides. Stage 4:

```
814 ST alloc  slot=0 id=15
816 ST upd    slot=0 id=15 addr=...21d8 data_v=1 data=aaaaaaaabbbbbbbb
817 ST upd    slot=1 id=16 addr=...21e0 data_v=1 data=ccccccccdddddddd
818 ST free   slot=0 id=15          <-- store leaves the LSQ
819 ST free   slot=1 id=16
820 LD query id=17 addr=...21d8 -> stall=0 fwd=0 data=0
```

The load queries **the same address** as store id=15, two cycles after that store was
freed. The LSQ therefore finds no match: it forwards nothing and stalls nothing, and
the load goes to memory.

Stores are freed from the LSQ **at commit**, but a committed store's data is still in
the LSU `store_buffer` and has not yet reached the cache. The LSQ's protection window
closes at commit while *visibility* lags behind it, and nothing covers the gap. Once
the store is gone from the queue, the load cannot be stalled or forwarded by it —
correctness in that window depends entirely on the `store_buffer`'s own byte-exact
forward, which is precisely what the LSQ comment says owns the data path.

This accounts for every observation, including the ones that killed earlier
hypotheses: **two live stores** put two entries in the `store_buffer`, lengthening the
drain so the load beats it; a **zero-cycle gap** puts the load immediately behind;
**one `nop`** gives the buffer the cycle it needs. It also explains why extending the
live mask failed — the store is already *gone* from the mask's source when the load
queries, so making the mask linger changes the wrong edge.

**Two corrections to the reading above, both from source.**

*The trace's `data=0` is not the load's result.* It is `stl_data_o`, the LSQ's own
forward value — and `g6lc_ooo_dispatch` leaves that port **unconnected**
(`.stl_data_o ()`), exactly as the LSQ comment says ("observability of … the same
predicate the store_buffer checks downstream"). So the trace shows what the *queue*
concluded, not what the load received. The load's data still comes from the LSU.

*That also rules out the next obvious candidate* — that the OoO path completes loads
from the LSQ's forward instead of the store buffer's. It does not; only
`stl_forward_o` propagates, and only as observability.

So the data path is the LSU's in both configurations, and `store_buffer`'s
`page_offset_matches_o` scans **both** the speculative and the commit queue plus the
incoming store, which on the face of it should cover the post-commit window in either
core. Why the OoO core nevertheless reads stale data is therefore **not yet
explained**, and the honest statement is that the LSQ trace narrowed *where* to look
without settling the mechanism.

##### The in-order/OoO comparison is confounded by `NrHarts`

Looking at the LSU side turned up something that undermines the control itself. Three
store-to-load forwarding protections in `store_buffer.sv` are gated on
**`CVA6Cfg.SuperscalarEn && CVA6Cfg.NrHarts > 1`**:

* the `fwd_keep` marking on a speculative entry a live load forwarded from,
* the companion rule that such an entry must drain rather than be cancelled (`G1ah`),
* the `g1ao_hold` fallback that re-supplies data when the byte merge finds nothing.

Their comments date them to the SMT2 work. Now compare the two models used throughout
this investigation:

| Config | `SuperscalarEn` | `NrHarts` | Those protections |
|---|---|---|---|
| `g6lc64_smt2` — the "in-order control" | 1 | **2** | **active** |
| `g6lc64_ooo_int` — the subject | 1 | **1** | **disabled** |

So the control and the subject differ in `NrHarts` as well as in `OoOEn`, and the
difference gates exactly the store-to-load machinery under suspicion. **Every "passes
in-order, fails in OoO" statement in this section is therefore confounded** — the
discriminating variable may be `NrHarts`, not out-of-order execution.

##### Confound tested — and the OoO conclusion survives

The clean control is `g6lc64_ooo_int` with **only `OoOEn` flipped to 0**: identical
`NrHarts=1`, `SuperscalarEn=1`, identical everything else, so the store-buffer
protections above stay disabled in *both* arms and the single free variable is
out-of-order execution.

| Stage | OoO (`OoOEn=1`) | In-order, same config (`OoOEn=0`) |
|---|---|---|
| 3 store → same-address load | PASS 866 | PASS 900 |
| 4 two stores, load the older | **FAILED** | **PASS 902** |
| 5 store/load loop | **HANG** | **PASS 1,041** |
| 10 one store, adjacent consumer | PASS 866 | PASS 900 |

Both failures disappear when `OoOEn` alone is cleared. So the `NrHarts` gating is a
real methodological hazard — the earlier `g6lc64_smt2` comparison genuinely was
confounded — but it is **not** the cause here: with a single-variable control, the
defects are in the **out-of-order path**, and the original conclusion stands on
evidence rather than on the accident of which baseline was convenient.

This also promotes stage 5 from "may or may not share a cause" to a second OoO defect:
it too passes in-order on the identical configuration.

##### Localised: the store buffer forwards the WRONG DATA under OoO

A temporary `load_unit` probe (since removed) logged the forward inputs for every
load, run on both arms of the `OoOEn`-only control. For the same address `…21d8`, the
same test, the same binary:

| Arm | `page_offset_matches` | `st_fwd_valid` | `st_fwd_be` | **`st_fwd_data`** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| in-order (passes) | 1 | 1 | full | **`aaaaaaaabbbbbbbb`** — correct |
| OoO (fails) | 1 | 1 | full | **`0000000000000001`** — wrong |

This relocates the defect decisively. The hazard detection is **not** broken: both arms
match the page offset, both raise a full-coverage forward. What differs is the **value
the store buffer supplies**. The OoO load is correctly told "an older store covers
your bytes" and is then handed the wrong bytes.

That invalidates the framing this investigation had been using for several rounds —
ordering, wakeup timing, and the age-gate-versus-`stl_stall` question were all
addressing whether the load is *held long enough*, when the load is in fact held
correctly and simply receives bad data. It also explains why the one-cycle `nop`
hides it: with a cycle of slack the entry is no longer being read in the state that
produces the bad merge.

##### RETRACTED: "the first store never reaches the store buffer"

**The section below is wrong and is kept only because the reasoning built on it runs
for several pages.** The claim came from a `grep … | tail -10` whose output was
truncated at the top, cutting off the very line that disproved it. Re-reading the same
log without the truncation:

```
817 PUSH paddr=…21d8 data=aaaaaaaabbbbbbbb tid=15
818 PUSH paddr=…21e0 data=ccccccccdddddddd tid=16
```

**Both stores are pushed into the store buffer, with correct addresses and correct
data.** A later `store_unit` FSM probe agrees independently: `st_valid=1` at 818 with
`paddr=…21d8` and again at 819 with `paddr=…21e0`. Nothing is dropped in dispatch, in
`lsu_bypass`, or in `store_unit`.

##### The load reads the WRONG ADDRESS — a stale base register

Re-reading the load-unit log without truncation removes the forwarding story as well.
There is **no** LSU access to `…21d8` anywhere near the test. What there is:

* the LSQ queries the test load (tid 17) at **`0x800221d8`** — correct,
* the LSU access one cycle earlier is at **`0x80022218`**,
* and `RESULT tid=17 data=0x1` follows it.

Those two addresses are not arbitrary. The test opens with `addi sp, sp, -64`:

| | sp | `8(sp)` |
|---|---|---|
| before the `addi` | `0x80022210` | **`0x80022218`** |
| after the `addi` | `0x800221d0` | **`0x800221d8`** |

**`0x80022218` is `8(sp)` computed with the pre-decrement `sp`.** The load's base
register is stale: it read `sp` from before the stack adjustment, so it addressed a
different word entirely and returned whatever lived there. The stores used the
*correct* `sp` — which is why the two stores land at `…21d8` / `…21e0` and the load
misses them both.

So this is not a store-to-load forwarding defect at any level. It is a **RAW hazard on
the load's `rs1` that the OoO operand path does not honour**, and the "full-coverage
forward of `0x1`" seen earlier is simply a correct forward for a *different* address.

It also explains the shape of the reproduction better than any previous theory: the
two stores are not the cause but the *timing*, spacing the load far enough from
`addi sp` to expose the window, which is equally why one `nop` changes the outcome.

Two paths compute this load's address and they **disagree** — dispatch's AGU (used for
the LSQ query, correct) and `issue_read_operands`' `operand_a` (used by the LSU,
stale). That disagreement is the defect, and it is squarely in the Phase 5/OoO operand
delivery this work touched.

**Confirmed.** The probe was re-run carrying the trans_id on the access line, so the
load is identified explicitly rather than by adjacency:

```
821 ACCESS tid=17 vaddr=0x80022218      <- stale, pre-decrement 8(sp)
822 RESULT tid=17 data=0x1
```

`tid 17` is the same load the LSQ queried at `0x800221d8`. The two address paths
provably disagree for one architectural instruction.

**Status: root cause established.** The OoO load path delivers a stale `rs1` to the
LSU while dispatch's AGU uses the correct value. Everything downstream — the "wrong
forwarded data", the two-store dependency, the one-`nop` sensitivity — follows from a
load issued against the wrong address.

##### FIXED: the scoreboard forward was overriding renamed operands

Both paths read the *same* signal (`op_a_agu`), so they could only diverge downstream —
and they do, at `issue_read_operands`:

```systemverilog
if (forward_rs1[i]) fu_data_n[i].operand_a = rs1_res[i];   // line ~928
```

The operand MUX takes `operand_a_regfile` (which correctly prefers the OoO PRF value)
and then lets the **legacy scoreboard forward overwrite it**. And renamed ops were
explicitly opted in to that:

```systemverilog
if (CVA6Cfg.OoOEn && issue_instr_i[i].ooo_renamed) begin
  // still allow scoreboard forward for same-cycle precision if valid
  if (rs1_has_raw[i] && rs1_valid[i]) forward_rs1[i] = 1'b1;
```

The scoreboard is an **in-order** structure that does not track renaming, so its idea
of "the latest producer of `sp`" can be stale for a renamed op — and it wins the MUX.
That is precisely the measured symptom: the load received the pre-`addi` `sp`.

Removing that opt-in (a renamed op's operands come from the PRF, which is
authoritative) fixes it:

| Stage | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| 3 store → same-address load | PASS 866 | PASS 866 |
| **4 two stores, load the older** | **FAILED** | **PASS 878** |
| 5 store/load loop | HANG | HANG *(still)* |
| 10 one store, adjacent consumer | PASS 866 | PASS 866 |

`ooo_ilp_chain` is unchanged at 1,109 cycles, so nothing regressed.

**The in-order path is unaffected**, verified behaviourally rather than by inspection:
the change sits inside `if (CVA6Cfg.OoOEn && …)`, and the qualified SMT2 dual-hart
OpenSBI boot re-run on a freshly built model gives **`strictDualPassed = true`,
333,635 / 8,932,406 retirements, 12,765,628 cycles** — identical to the reference.

##### Defect 2 (loop hang): cause confirmed — the IQ age gate deadlocks

The same isolation method narrows it in four cheap runs. Neither ingredient alone
hangs:

| Loop contains | Result |
|---|---|
| no memory | PASS 959 |
| store only | PASS 956 |
| load only | PASS 956 |
| **store + load, same address** | **HANG** |

And the trigger is depth, not the dependence itself:

| Iterations | Result |
|---|---:|
| 2 | PASS 869 |
| 3 | PASS 877 |
| **4** | **HANG** |
| 16 | HANG |

Four iterations is roughly 28 instructions plus prologue against
`NrScoreboardEntries = 32` — the window fills.

**Confirmed by intervention.** Disabling the IQ's `older_st` age gate (unsound: it
removes memory ordering, used only as a diagnostic and reverted immediately) makes
both loops complete:

| | gate enabled | gate disabled |
|---|---|---|
| 4-iteration loop | HANG | **PASS 852** |
| 16-iteration loop | HANG | **PASS 934** |

So the deadlock is that gate. The mechanism it implies: a load is held because some
store looks **older** by the circular compare

```systemverilog
(TRANS_ID_BITS'(s) - commit_ptr_i) < (q_chain[e].sbe.trans_id - commit_ptr_i)
```

while in-order commit cannot retire that store ahead of the load — so neither can
make progress. A raw modular subtraction only orders correctly while every live
trans_id lies within one window of `commit_ptr`; once the loop spreads them far enough
around the circle, a younger store compares as older. That is exactly why the hang
appears at the depth where the window fills, and why it needs both a store and a load
(the load's own stall is what lets the window fill in the first place).

**…and then the state dump overturned that reading.** Before designing a fix, the
queue was dumped periodically while stuck:

```
[iqd] 40014 cnt=0 commit_ptr=4 st_live=00000000
[iqd] 60014 cnt=0 commit_ptr=4 st_live=00000000
[iqd] 80014 cnt=0 commit_ptr=4 st_live=00000000
```

**The IQ is empty and no store is live.** Nothing is being held by the age gate at
all — the pipeline is fully drained with `commit_ptr` frozen at 4, and the harness
reports "a core ran and then stopped retiring" with a maximum *intra-run* retirement
gap of only 20 cycles. The core executed, then stopped dead.

So the age-gate conclusion does not survive: an empty queue cannot be deadlocked by a
gate that only blocks queue entries. **The disabling experiment changed global timing
and evidently masked the fault rather than removing its cause** — the same way one
`nop` masked defect 1. A passing result from an intervention is not proof of mechanism
when the intervention perturbs timing everywhere.

What the dump does establish: the hang is **not** in the IQ, not in store-to-load
ordering, and not in the LSQ — those are all idle.

Three further probes closed it out. The LSQ **load** queue is empty too, so no load is
outstanding. And dispatch reports:

```
disp_valid=01 ack=00 can_go=0 ren_stall=0 rob_full=1 iq_full=0 lsq_block=0 flush=0
```

**The front end is offering instructions; dispatch refuses because the ROB is full** —
with the IQ empty, both LSQ queues empty, and nothing in flight anywhere. Four
iterations is ~28 instructions plus prologue against a 32-entry window, which is
exactly where a ROB that never frees entries would wedge.

The chain is therefore: the scoreboard head (`commit_ptr = 4`) never completes →
commit never acks → `g6lc_rob` retires on `retire_ack_i (commit_ack_i)` so it never
frees → `rob_full` → dispatch blocks → the pipeline drains and stays drained.

So the defect is **an instruction that issues and executes but whose completion never
reaches the commit point**. Dumping the scoreboard head names it exactly:

```
[sbd] head=4 issued=1 cancelled=0 done(valid)=0 fu=4 op=18 pc=0x800001ec
```

`fu = 4` is **CTRL_FLOW** and `op = 18` is **NE** — this is the loop's `bnez`
back-edge. **A branch was issued and never completed.** It sits in the scoreboard
`issued` and not `cancelled`, but never marked valid/done, so commit can never pass
it.

That is a coherent end to the chain and it fits every constraint: the loop is the only
construct that executes the same backward branch repeatedly, ≥4 iterations is where
the predictor first mispredicts it, and a branch stuck un-completed explains a drained
pipeline far better than any memory-ordering story — the memory ops all finished, which
is exactly why the IQ and both LSQ queues are empty.

**A structural mismatch found while chasing this — real, but not the cause.**
`issue_read_operands` derives port-*p* eligibility from `issue_instr_i[p-1]`:

```systemverilog
for (int unsigned p = 1; p < CVA6Cfg.NrIssuePorts; p++) begin
  fus_busy[p] = fus_busy[p-1];
  fus_busy[p].csr = 1'b1;              // CSR/CVXIF port-0 only
  unique case (issue_instr_i[p-1].fu)  // <- the PREVIOUS PORT's instruction
    CTRL_FLOW: if (CVA6Cfg.SuperscalarEn) fus_busy[p] = '1;
```

That is in-order multi-issue logic: it assumes the two ports hold instructions
**adjacent in program order**. The OoO IQ presents any two ready entries, so the
assumption does not hold, and the port-1 mask is not a sound filter under `OoOEn`.
The FLU (branch/CSR/mult) lives only on port 0, so with the FLU ready
`fus_busy[1].ctrl_flow` is 0 — a branch granted to port 1 would be acked, dropped from
the queue, and never executed. That matches the observed `issued && !done` branch
exactly.

**Tested, and it is not the cause.** Restricting FLU-only ops to issue slot 0 and
serializing after a branch (`NrIssuePorts = 2`, so the guard was live) changed
nothing: stages 5 and 15 still hang and `ooo_mem_dep` still times out, with stage 4
still passing at 878 cycles. Reverted, since an unvalidated issue restriction costs
bandwidth for no measured benefit.

The mismatch is recorded as a **latent soundness risk** for when OoO and superscalar
issue are both live — it is simply not what wedges this loop.

##### The FLU retires one trans_id per cycle; OoO can present two

Extrapolating the branch's execution path end to end
(`IQ → issue_read_operands → ex_stage → branch_unit → flu_* → scoreboard`) exposes a
sharper mismatch than the issue-port one, and this time the design states the
invariant itself.

`issue_read_operands` emits **per-port** vectors (`alu_valid_o`, `branch_valid_o`,
`csr_valid_o`, `aes_valid_o`), and `ex_stage` combines them:

```systemverilog
assign one_cycle_select = alu_valid_i | branch_valid_i | csr_valid_i | aes_valid_i;
...
assign flu_valid_o = |one_cycle_select | mult_valid;      // ONE valid
...
flu_trans_id_o = one_cycle_data.trans_id;                  // ONE trans_id
if (|branch_valid_i) flu_trans_id_o = branch_data.trans_id;   // branch wins
else if (alu_valid_i[0]) flu_trans_id_o = fu_data_i[0].trans_id;
```

`one_cycle_select` is a vector; `flu_valid_o`/`flu_trans_id_o` are scalars. **If two
FLU-bound one-cycle ops are selected in the same cycle, exactly one trans_id is
written back and the other completion is lost** — and a lost completion is precisely
an entry left `issued && !done`, which wedges commit, fills the ROB and drains the
pipeline. That is the measured state.

The invariant is documented in `ex_stage` itself: *"If port0 is ALU and a later port
is also one-cycle, ALU0 must write port0's tid … Port1+ ALU uses ALU2 / FPU_WB."* The
design assumes **at most one FLU-bound one-cycle op per cycle**, achieved by routing
port-1 ALU work to ALU2 and by the in-order `fus_busy` chain serialising the rest.
The OoO IQ selects any two ready entries and therefore breaks that assumption — the
same root as the port-eligibility mismatch, one level further down.

**An unfinished check on the earlier experiment.** Forcing branches to slot 0 should,
by this analysis, have let the branch win the FLU (`fus_busy[1] = '1` after a port-0
CTRL_FLOW blocks the sibling). It did not clear the hang — but I concluded "the fix
failed" from the hang persisting **without re-probing which instruction was stuck**.
If the analysis here is right, the stuck entry should have *changed* (to the sibling
one-cycle op that lost the FLU), which is a different defect surfacing rather than the
same one persisting. Re-running the scoreboard-head dump with that guard in place is
the immediate next step, and it distinguishes the two cleanly.

**That theory also fails on a closer read.** `fus_busy[p] = '1` is applied after an
ALU *or* a CTRL_FLOW on port `p-1` under `SuperscalarEn` ("One ALU issue/cycle under
SS until dual-WB clean on OpenSBI"), and `fu_busy[i]` gates `issue_ack[i]`. So
`issue_read_operands` already serialises to one FLU-bound op per cycle whatever the IQ
presents — two FLU completions cannot be selected together. Five hypotheses down.

##### CONFIRMED by configuration: the hang is an OoO × dual-issue interaction

Rather than a sixth site-specific theory, the *class* was tested directly — the
integer OoO config with `NrIssuePorts = 1` and `SuperscalarEn = 0`, everything else
unchanged:

| Test | 2-issue OoO | single-issue OoO |
|---|---|---|
| stage 4 (two stores, older load) | PASS 878 | PASS 942 |
| stage 5 (16-iteration store/load loop) | **HANG** | **PASS 1,069** |
| stage 15 (4-iteration loop) | **HANG** | **PASS 938** |
| `ooo_mem_dep` | **TIMEOUT 400,000** | **PASS 1,151** |
| `ooo_ilp_chain` | PASS 1,109 | PASS 1,380 |
| stages 3, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 16 | pass | pass |

Every stage and both directed tests pass at single issue. The defect is therefore
**not** in the OoO core proper — rename, IQ, ROB, LSQ and the memory path all behave —
but in the interaction between OoO instruction selection and the issue/execute
structures that assume the two ports carry program-order-adjacent instructions. The
two mismatches catalogued above (`fus_busy[p]` derived from `issue_instr_i[p-1]`, and
the scalar `flu_valid_o`/`flu_trans_id_o` behind a vector `one_cycle_select`) are
members of that class even though neither alone is the trigger.

##### Two defects fixed as a consequence

1. **`iq_issue_valid` was used before its declaration** in `g6lc_ooo_dispatch`
   (the IQ instantiation consumes it ~70 lines above where the FP work declared it).
   Verilator accepts this; **slang does not**, so *synthesis of `g6lc64_ooo_int` was
   broken* by the FP-operand work and the breakage was invisible to every simulation
   run. Declaration moved up. `g6lc64_ooo_int` now passes **lint (10 warnings, baseline
   11) and synthesis (clean, 2 warnings)** again.

2. Pre-existing, found by the single-issue experiment and **not yet fixed**:
   `issue_read_operands.sv:1304/1337/1341` index `raddr_pack[2]` unconditionally in
   the Zacas CASQ phase-1 path. With `NrIssuePorts = 1` the pack is `[1:0]` and
   elaboration fails (`cannot refer to element 2`). So single-issue OoO **simulates
   correctly but cannot be synthesised** until that indexing is bounded — which is why
   the config is left at 2-issue rather than shipped as the working baseline.

Note this is squarely a **pre-existing OoO defect**, not Phase 5 fallout: stage 5 hung
before the operand fix, the ROB completes on `wb_valid_i` matched by `trans_id`
untouched by any FP edit, and no FP is present in this configuration at all.

Two earlier readings of this same hang — "the age gate deadlocks" and, before it,
"store-to-load forwarding" — were both wrong. The consistent lesson is that each was
adopted from a plausible mechanism plus one supporting experiment, where the
experiment perturbed timing globally. The state dumps, which observe rather than
perturb, have been right every time.

Defect 1's fix is unaffected by this — it was confirmed by a targeted, tid-tagged
measurement and by stage 4 passing, not by a timing perturbation.

Method note for the next pass: every conclusion in this investigation that came from a
`tail`/`head`-truncated grep needs re-checking against the full log. This is the second
self-inflicted measurement error in the session, after the `s11` block-id carrier.

---

##### (superseded) ROOT CAUSE: under OoO the first of two close stores never reaches the store buffer

A push probe on `store_buffer`'s speculative-queue insert, run on both arms of the
`OoOEn`-only control with the identical binary:

| Arm | Pushes seen |
|---|---|
| in-order (passes) | `21d8 <- aaaaaaaabbbbbbbb` (tid 24) **and** `21e0 <- ccccccccdddddddd` (tid 25) |
| OoO (fails) | **only** `21e0 <- ccccccccdddddddd` (tid 16) |

**The first store is lost.** It is not mis-ordered, not mis-forwarded, not early — it
never arrives. The LSQ had it correctly (the earlier trace shows slot 0, id 15,
`addr=…21d8`, `data_v=1`, `data=aaaaaaaabbbbbbbb`), so the store resolved inside the
OoO queue and was then dropped on the way to the LSU.

That single fact explains every observation in this investigation:

* **wrong forwarded data** — the buffer never held `aaaa…`, so a later load of that
  address merges whatever stale entry is there (`0x1`) with full byte coverage;
* **needs two live stores** — with one store there is nothing to drop;
* **one `nop` hides it** — separating the two stores lets both through;
* **the loop hang (stage 5)** — repeated drops, consistent with a structure that never
  drains, though that link is not yet proven;
* and it retrospectively explains why every ordering-side hypothesis failed: the load
  path was working correctly the whole time.

Note the LSQ "protection window closes at commit" reading recorded above is **not** the
cause. It described a real property of the queue, but the defect is upstream of it.

**First candidate mechanism tested and eliminated.** The dispatch side is correctly
per-port (`agu_valid[p]`, `st_data[p]`, `st_data_id[p]` are all vectors; the only
scalar, `vaddr_x`, is written and consumed inside one loop iteration). But the LSU has
a single memory pipe and the IQ's select loop constrains only *loads* (behind older
stores) — nothing stops it granting two memory ops in one cycle. That looked like the
drop.
